People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 43 / chief_executive / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I’m probably making too much of it, but I’ve been hearing people talking about me when they aren’t there. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and it’s so clear that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard anything. I know that probably sounds strange… but that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing the only unusual perceptions you're experiencing, or are you seeing, smelling, tasting, or feeling things that aren't there as well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. It’s mainly the voices. The people talking—like through the conference room walls, or from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds so real, usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and sometimes I’ll answer them before I realize no one else heard it. I know that sounds… not great, but I’m not seeing or smelling anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing constant, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second, if that’s what you mean. But it happens enough that it’s hard to ignore. Mostly at work, like I’ll hear them through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining things. I know that sounds dramatic… but when it happens, it’s really clear. Sometimes I answer before I catch myself.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever talk about what you're thinking, or are they mostly discussing your actions or things related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s about work, I think… about what I’m doing or supposedly doing wrong. Like the board members or staff are saying I’m ruining the company. I don’t know that they’re talking about my thoughts exactly—more like they’re commenting on me, if that makes sense. It comes through the walls or even my phone when it’s off, and it sounds so real that I’ll answer before I realize… no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever tell you to do something or influence what you do?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do things, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like they’re talking about me—board members or staff, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes it’s so clear, like through the conference room walls or even my phone when it’s off, that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard it. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I’m probably making too much of it, but I’ve been hearing people talking about me when they aren’t there. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and it’s so clear that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard anything. I know that probably sounds strange… but that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing the only unusual perceptions you're experiencing, or are you seeing, smelling, tasting, or feeling things that aren't there as well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. It’s mainly the voices. The people talking—like through the conference room walls, or from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds so real, usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and sometimes I’ll answer them before I realize no one else heard it. I know that sounds… not great, but I’m not seeing or smelling anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing constant, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second, if that’s what you mean. But it happens enough that it’s hard to ignore. Mostly at work, like I’ll hear them through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining things. I know that sounds dramatic… but when it happens, it’s really clear. Sometimes I answer before I catch myself.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever talk about what you're thinking, or are they mostly discussing your actions or things related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s about work, I think… about what I’m doing or supposedly doing wrong. Like the board members or staff are saying I’m ruining the company. I don’t know that they’re talking about my thoughts exactly—more like they’re commenting on me, if that makes sense. It comes through the walls or even my phone when it’s off, and it sounds so real that I’ll answer before I realize… no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever tell you to do something or influence what you do?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do things, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like they’re talking about me—board members or staff, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes it’s so clear, like through the conference room walls or even my phone when it’s off, that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard it. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.

I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Like if I suddenly stand up in a meeting or start rearranging pens or name cards into a pattern, it’s not because I think it’s appropriate exactly. It just sort of gives my hands something to do.

But while it’s happening, I guess it feels a little like I’m trying to get control back, even if it doesn’t really work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I’m probably making too much of it, but I’ve been hearing people talking about me when they aren’t there. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and it’s so clear that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard anything. I know that probably sounds strange… but that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing the only unusual perceptions you're experiencing, or are you seeing, smelling, tasting, or feeling things that aren't there as well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. It’s mainly the voices. The people talking—like through the conference room walls, or from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds so real, usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and sometimes I’ll answer them before I realize no one else heard it. I know that sounds… not great, but I’m not seeing or smelling anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing constant, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second, if that’s what you mean. But it happens enough that it’s hard to ignore. Mostly at work, like I’ll hear them through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining things. I know that sounds dramatic… but when it happens, it’s really clear. Sometimes I answer before I catch myself.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever talk about what you're thinking, or are they mostly discussing your actions or things related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s about work, I think… about what I’m doing or supposedly doing wrong. Like the board members or staff are saying I’m ruining the company. I don’t know that they’re talking about my thoughts exactly—more like they’re commenting on me, if that makes sense. It comes through the walls or even my phone when it’s off, and it sounds so real that I’ll answer before I realize… no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever tell you to do something or influence what you do?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do things, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like they’re talking about me—board members or staff, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes it’s so clear, like through the conference room walls or even my phone when it’s off, that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard it. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I’m probably making too much of it, but I’ve been hearing people talking about me when they aren’t there. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and it’s so clear that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard anything. I know that probably sounds strange… but that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing the only unusual perceptions you're experiencing, or are you seeing, smelling, tasting, or feeling things that aren't there as well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. It’s mainly the voices. The people talking—like through the conference room walls, or from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds so real, usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and sometimes I’ll answer them before I realize no one else heard it. I know that sounds… not great, but I’m not seeing or smelling anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing constant, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second, if that’s what you mean. But it happens enough that it’s hard to ignore. Mostly at work, like I’ll hear them through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff talking about me, saying I’m ruining things. I know that sounds dramatic… but when it happens, it’s really clear. Sometimes I answer before I catch myself.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever talk about what you're thinking, or are they mostly discussing your actions or things related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s about work, I think… about what I’m doing or supposedly doing wrong. Like the board members or staff are saying I’m ruining the company. I don’t know that they’re talking about my thoughts exactly—more like they’re commenting on me, if that makes sense. It comes through the walls or even my phone when it’s off, and it sounds so real that I’ll answer before I realize… no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever tell you to do something or influence what you do?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do things, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like they’re talking about me—board members or staff, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes it’s so clear, like through the conference room walls or even my phone when it’s off, that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard it. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.\n\nThen I’ll do something before I’ve really sorted out what’s happening, like answer them or start fussing with things in the room. Afterward I realize no one else seemed to hear it, and I feel embarrassed. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it quite right, but yes, it does feel like it’s a reaction to what they’re saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these odd things, like straightening pens, helps you feel better or more in control when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.\n\nThen I’ll do something before I’ve really sorted out what’s happening, like answer them or start fussing with things in the room. Afterward I realize no one else seemed to hear it, and I feel embarrassed. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it quite right, but yes, it does feel like it’s a reaction to what they’re saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these odd things, like straightening pens, helps you feel better or more in control when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Like if I suddenly stand up in a meeting or start rearranging pens or name cards into a pattern, it’s not because I think it’s appropriate exactly. It just sort of gives my hands something to do.\n\nOr I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, and afterward I know it made no sense for the room. I feel embarrassed about it. But while it’s happening, I guess it feels a little like I’m trying to get control back, even if it doesn’t really work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’d like to say no, that I’m pretty clear when that’s not happening. Sometimes I do feel… a little scattered, but I’ve also got a lot on my mind, so I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nI haven’t really noticed my thoughts being hard to follow on their own. If I’m not hearing anything, I think I can usually keep up okay. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your thoughts or making sense of what's going on when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in any big way. When there aren’t voices, I feel like I can usually follow what’s happening and think through things normally enough.\n\nI mean, I might get stressed or a little distracted, but I wouldn’t say I can’t make sense of things. I’m trying to answer honestly—if there’s something more specific you mean by “organizing my thoughts,” I can try to explain better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.\n\nThen I’ll do something before I’ve really sorted out what’s happening, like answer them or start fussing with things in the room. Afterward I realize no one else seemed to hear it, and I feel embarrassed. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it quite right, but yes, it does feel like it’s a reaction to what they’re saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these odd things, like straightening pens, helps you feel better or more in control when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Like if I suddenly stand up in a meeting or start rearranging pens or name cards into a pattern, it’s not because I think it’s appropriate exactly. It just sort of gives my hands something to do.\n\nOr I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, and afterward I know it made no sense for the room. I feel embarrassed about it. But while it’s happening, I guess it feels a little like I’m trying to get control back, even if it doesn’t really work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’d like to say no, that I’m pretty clear when that’s not happening. Sometimes I do feel… a little scattered, but I’ve also got a lot on my mind, so I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nI haven’t really noticed my thoughts being hard to follow on their own. If I’m not hearing anything, I think I can usually keep up okay. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your thoughts or making sense of what's going on when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in any big way. When there aren’t voices, I feel like I can usually follow what’s happening and think through things normally enough.\n\nI mean, I might get stressed or a little distracted, but I wouldn’t say I can’t make sense of things. I’m trying to answer honestly—if there’s something more specific you mean by “organizing my thoughts,” I can try to explain better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things, like straightening pens, or would you be able to stop yourself if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I could stop myself, because I know how it sounds, and I’m not trying to make a scene or anything. But in the moment it feels… almost automatic.\n\nLike in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up, or start lining up pens or name cards into some kind of pattern before I’ve really decided to. Or I’ll repeat what someone just said, or put my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward I can see it didn’t fit the situation at all, and I feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo… maybe I can sometimes catch it if I notice early enough, but a lot of the time it feels like it happens before I’ve fully chosen it. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. Not exactly… I mean, I don’t think I’m doing it to make some feeling go away, like anxiety or pressure. It’s more that I suddenly notice I’m already doing it.

In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.\n\nThen I’ll do something before I’ve really sorted out what’s happening, like answer them or start fussing with things in the room. Afterward I realize no one else seemed to hear it, and I feel embarrassed. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it quite right, but yes, it does feel like it’s a reaction to what they’re saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these odd things, like straightening pens, helps you feel better or more in control when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Like if I suddenly stand up in a meeting or start rearranging pens or name cards into a pattern, it’s not because I think it’s appropriate exactly. It just sort of gives my hands something to do.\n\nOr I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, and afterward I know it made no sense for the room. I feel embarrassed about it. But while it’s happening, I guess it feels a little like I’m trying to get control back, even if it doesn’t really work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’d like to say no, that I’m pretty clear when that’s not happening. But in the moment it feels… almost automatic.\n\nLike in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up, or start lining up pens or name cards into some kind of pattern before I’ve really decided to. Or I’ll repeat what someone just said, or put my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward I can see it didn’t fit the situation at all, and I feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo… maybe I can sometimes catch it if I notice early enough, but a lot of the time it feels like it happens before I’ve fully chosen it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things, like straightening pens, because something bad would happen if you didn't?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like that. I’m not thinking, “If I don’t line these up, something bad will happen.” It’s more like I suddenly do it before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nLike I’ll be in a meeting or at a gallery thing and I’ll stand up, or start moving pens or name cards into some pattern, or repeat something someone just said. It just… happens, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things to get rid of an uncomfortable feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly… I mean, I don’t think I’m doing it to make some feeling go away, like anxiety or pressure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, I hope I make sense most of the time. I try to be careful with how I say things, especially at work or with people I don’t know well.

Sometimes people look confused, and then I’ll realize maybe I didn’t explain it in the right order, or I skipped a step. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t say it’s all the time… at least, I don’t think so.

Do I seem like I’m making sense right now?

It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and it’s so clear that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard anything. I know that probably sounds strange… but that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing the only unusual perceptions you're experiencing, or are you seeing, smelling, tasting, or feeling things that aren't there as well?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. It sounds so real, usually board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company, and sometimes I’ll answer them before I realize no one else heard it. I know that sounds… not great, but I’m not seeing or smelling anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices you're hearing constant, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes I answer before I catch myself.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever talk about what you're thinking, or are they mostly discussing your actions or things related to your work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s about work, I think… about what I’m doing or supposedly doing wrong. It comes through the walls or even my phone when it’s off, and it sounds so real that I’ll answer before I realize… no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you're hearing ever tell you to do something or influence what you do?\n\nPatient: Not really telling me to do things, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more like they’re talking about me—board members or staff, saying I’m ruining the company. Sometimes it’s so clear, like through the conference room walls or even my phone when it’s off, that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard it. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.\n\nThen I’ll do something before I’ve really sorted out what’s happening, like answer them or start fussing with things in the room. Afterward I realize no one else seemed to hear it, and I feel embarrassed. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it quite right, but yes, it does feel like it’s a reaction to what they’re saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these odd things, like straightening pens, helps you feel better or more in control when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Like if I suddenly stand up in a meeting or start rearranging pens or name cards into a pattern, it’s not because I think it’s appropriate exactly. It just sort of gives my hands something to do.\n\nOr I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, and afterward I know it made no sense for the room. I feel embarrassed about it. But while it’s happening, I guess it feels a little like I’m trying to get control back, even if it doesn’t really work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’d like to say no, that I’m pretty clear when that’s not happening. Sometimes I do feel… a little scattered, but I’ve also got a lot on my mind, so I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nI haven’t really noticed my thoughts being hard to follow on their own. If I’m not hearing anything, I think I can usually keep up okay. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your thoughts or making sense of what's going on when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in any big way. When there aren’t voices, I feel like I can usually follow what’s happening and think through things normally enough.\n\nI mean, I might get stressed or a little distracted, but I wouldn’t say I can’t make sense of things. I’m trying to answer honestly—if there’s something more specific you mean by “organizing my thoughts,” I can try to explain better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things, like straightening pens, or would you be able to stop yourself if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I could stop myself, because I know how it sounds, and I’m not trying to make a scene or anything. Sometimes, yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I do get kind of stuck.

I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. Sometimes it’s so clear, like through the conference room walls or even my phone when it’s off, that I’ll answer back before I realize no one else heard it. But it’s not like they’re giving me instructions, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices are coming from inside your mind or from outside?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s coming from outside, honestly… not like a thought in my head. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. I know that probably sounds strange, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but in the moment it’s so clear that I answer before I realize no one else heard it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are not your own or that someone or something is controlling your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, my thoughts feel like mine, as far as I can tell. I haven’t noticed anyone controlling my mind or putting thoughts in there. Is that the kind of thing you mean? Because it’s more that I get confused by what I’m hearing, but I wouldn’t say my mind is being controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to express your thoughts or keep track of what you're saying when these voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I lose track that badly. I try to keep myself together, especially when I’m talking to someone. Sometimes if it’s happening at the same time, I might pause or forget where I was for a second, but I can usually get back to it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or behavior since you started hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’ve just noticed I act a bit… odd sometimes in public situations. Like in meetings or at gallery events, I might suddenly stand up and start straightening pens or moving name cards into some kind of pattern, or I’ll repeat something someone just said without really meaning to.\n\nSometimes I’ve even caught myself holding my hands up like I’m presenting something, but there wasn’t any reason for it. Afterward I realize it didn’t really fit the room, and I feel embarrassed. Is that the kind of behavior change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said, in response to the voices you're hearing?\n\nPatient: I think… sometimes, yes, but I’m a little embarrassed saying that. It’s not like I’m trying to make a scene or anything. In meetings or at gallery events, if I’m hearing them, I might suddenly stand up and start lining up pens or moving name cards into a pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said.\n\nOther times I’ll catch myself with my hands up, like I’m presenting something, even though there’s no reason for it. Afterward I know it didn’t make sense for the room. I don’t always know if it’s exactly because of the voices, but it does seem to happen around the same time. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voices are directly telling you or influencing you to do these odd things, like straightening pens or repeating what someone said?\n\nPatient: Maybe not like… a command exactly. It’s more that I hear them talking about me, very clearly, and I get rattled. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off. It sounds like board members or staff saying I’m ruining the company.\n\nAnd then I’ll react before I’ve really thought it through—sometimes I answer them out loud, and only after that I realize no one else heard anything. So I don’t know if that counts as influencing me, but it feels like it pushes me into doing things. Is that an okay way to explain it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're doing these odd things, like straightening pens, as a direct reaction to what the voices are saying about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I don’t mean it like I’m deciding to be disruptive. It’s more like I hear them so clearly—through the conference room walls, or even from my phone when it’s off—saying things like I’m ruining the company, and I get startled or defensive.\n\nThen I’ll do something before I’ve really sorted out what’s happening, like answer them or start fussing with things in the room. Afterward I realize no one else seemed to hear it, and I feel embarrassed. I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it quite right, but yes, it does feel like it’s a reaction to what they’re saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think doing these odd things, like straightening pens, helps you feel better or more in control when you're hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe in the moment it feels like I’m doing something to steady myself, if that makes sense. Like if I suddenly stand up in a meeting or start rearranging pens or name cards into a pattern, it’s not because I think it’s appropriate exactly. It just sort of gives my hands something to do.\n\nOr I’ll repeat a phrase someone just said, or hold my hands up like I’m presenting something, and afterward I know it made no sense for the room. I feel embarrassed about it. But while it’s happening, I guess it feels a little like I’m trying to get control back, even if it doesn’t really work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’d like to say no, that I’m pretty clear when that’s not happening. Sometimes I do feel… a little scattered, but I’ve also got a lot on my mind, so I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nI haven’t really noticed my thoughts being hard to follow on their own. If I’m not hearing anything, I think I can usually keep up okay. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your thoughts or making sense of what's going on when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in any big way. When there aren’t voices, I feel like I can usually follow what’s happening and think through things normally enough.\n\nI mean, I might get stressed or a little distracted, but I wouldn’t say I can’t make sense of things. I’m trying to answer honestly—if there’s something more specific you mean by “organizing my thoughts,” I can try to explain better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things, like straightening pens, or would you be able to stop yourself if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I want to say I could stop myself, because I know how it sounds, and I’m not trying to make a scene or anything. But in the moment it feels… almost automatic.\n\nLike in a meeting or at a gallery event, I’ll suddenly stand up, or start lining up pens or name cards into some kind of pattern before I’ve really decided to. Or I’ll repeat what someone just said, or put my hands up like I’m presenting something. Afterward I can see it didn’t fit the situation at all, and I feel really embarrassed.\n\nSo… maybe I can sometimes catch it if I notice early enough, but a lot of the time it feels like it happens before I’ve fully chosen it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things, like straightening pens, because something bad would happen if you didn't?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like that. I’m not thinking, “If I don’t line these up, something bad will happen.” It’s more like I suddenly do it before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nLike I’ll be in a meeting or at a gallery thing and I’ll stand up, or start moving pens or name cards into some pattern, or repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll even raise my hands like I’m presenting, and then afterward I realize it didn’t make sense for the room at all.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. But it’s not because I believe something terrible will happen if I don’t do it. It just… happens, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do these odd things to get rid of an uncomfortable feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly… I mean, I don’t think I’m doing it to make some feeling go away, like anxiety or pressure. It’s more that I suddenly notice I’m already doing it.\n\nLike I’ll stand up in a meeting, or start moving pens or name cards into a pattern at an event, or repeat something someone just said. Sometimes I’ll put my hands up like I’m about to present something, and then afterward I’m thinking, why did I do that? That didn’t fit at all.\n\nI know it probably sounds odd. I’m sorry. I’m trying to be clear—it’s not really to relieve a feeling, it just sort of happens, and then I feel embarrassed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to express yourself in a way that makes sense to others when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I hope I make sense most of the time. I try to be careful with how I say things, especially at work or with people I don’t know well.\n\nSometimes people look confused, and then I’ll realize maybe I didn’t explain it in the right order, or I skipped a step. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I wouldn’t say it’s all the time… at least, I don’t think so.\n\nDo I seem like I’m making sense right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words when you're not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I do get kind of stuck.\n\nI used to be the one driving every idea, talking through plans, calling people after openings, keeping everything moving. Now I can sit at my desk for hours just staring at the agenda and not really know how to start saying what I mean. Or I barely say anything to my staff, or even to my husband, and it’s not because I’m trying to be rude.\n\nIt’s more like the words don’t come, or there’s nothing behind them. And the strange part is I don’t feel as much about the work as I used to, even though I know I loved it. Does that answer what you’re asking?"
